    "newString": "\\subsection{The Symbolic Mirage Hypothesis}\n\\label{sec:mirage}\n\nWe hypothesize that self-extracted symbolic structures are not independent verification signals but projections of the generating model's distributional biases. This \\textbf{Symbolic Mirage} is specific to self-extracted constraints; symbolic methods employing independently sourced constraints remain effective (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}).\n\n\\paragraph{Definition (Symbolic Mirage).}\nFor model $M$ on task $T$, the Symbolic Mirage is the systematic alignment between self-extracted constraints and the model's own answer, measured by the \\emph{Bias Ratio}:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mirage}\n\\text{BR}(M,T) = \\frac{P(c \\text{ supports } a_M \\mid a_M \\neq a^*)}{P(c \\text{ supports } a^* \\mid a_M \\neq a^*)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $c$ is a self-extracted constraint, $a_M$ the model's answer, and $a^*$ the ground truth. $\\text{BR} > 1$ indicates constraints are projections of model bias rather than independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{Proposition 1 (Accuracy Ceiling).}\nGiven $\\text{BR} > 1$, the maximum accuracy improvement of any same-model constraint aggregation over SC is bounded by $\\Delta_{\\max} \\leq \\text{Oracle\\_Gap} / \\text{BR}$.\n\\emph{Rationale}: oracle constraints achieve $+14.22$\\,pp (Qwen-14B FOLIO); self-extracted constraints have signal diluted by factor $1/\\text{BR}$ due to bias contamination. \\emph{Prediction}: $\\Delta_{\\max} \\leq 14.22 / 4.97 \\approx 2.86$\\,pp. \\emph{Observed}: mean $\\Delta$ across seeds $= +2.12$\\,pp; max observed $= +3.43$\\,pp (single seed, $p = 0.092$ n.s.). All 25+ same-model methods fall within this bound (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Proposition 2 (Effective Independence).}\nWith $K$ traces and inter-trace agreement $\\kappa$, the effective sample size is $K_{\\text{eff}} = K / (1 + (K{-}1) \\cdot \\kappa)$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}). At $K{=}12$: $\\kappa = 0.50$ (Mistral) yields $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.85$; $\\kappa = 0.74$ (Qwen-14B) yields $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.31$. \\emph{Implication}: increasing $K$ has diminishing returns; with $K_{\\text{eff}} < 2$, twelve traces provide almost no aggregation benefit beyond a single pair.\n\n\\paragraph{Proposition 3 (Process-$\\kappa$ as Mirage Detector).}\nProcess-level similarity (cosine $0.69$) far exceeds answer-level agreement ($\\kappa = 0.41$), with negligible correlation ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$). \\emph{Implication}: traces follow shared reasoning paths $\\to$ extracted constraints inherit shared biases $\\to$ the mirage is unavoidable within same-model sampling (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).\n\n\\paragraph{Proposition 4 (Cross-Architecture Escape).}\nIf verifier $V$ has different inductive bias from generator $M$, then $\\text{BR}_{\\text{cross}}(M,V) < \\text{BR}_{\\text{same}}(M,M)$. \\emph{Empirical}: DeBERTa (encoder-only, NLI-finetuned) as verifier yields the only statistically significant improvement ($+3.43$\\,pp, $p = 0.039$), with per-label complementarity ($+33$\\,pp on negation questions). Cross-architecture verification partially escapes the mirage because $V$'s biases are structurally different from $M$'s (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Background \\& Setup}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Self-Consistency and the Condorcet Independence Assumption}\n\\label{sec:condorcet}\n\nSelf-Consistency (SC) \\citep{wang2023sc} samples $K$ reasoning traces and selects the majority answer. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees that if $K$ independent voters are each correct with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth as $K \\to \\infty$ \\citep{austensmith1996}. LLM traces violate this independence: all $K$ samples come from the same model with only temperature as variation. Following \\citet{ladha1992correlated} and the design-effect framework of \\citet{kish1965survey}, we define effective sample size using Fleiss' $\\kappa$ \\citep{fleiss1971kappa} as proxy for intraclass correlation:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:effk}\nK_{\\text{eff}} = \\frac{K}{1 + (K - 1) \\cdot \\kappa}.\n\\end{equation}\nIn our experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82 across instruction-tuned pairs (0.91 for thinking mode), yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} \\approx 1.2\\text{--}3.6$ for $K = 12$. Note that $\\kappa$ is a qualitative proxy for the intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC); we use $K_{\\text{eff}}$ as an interpretive indicator rather than a precise statistical estimator. This \\emph{independence bottleneck} motivates our investigation.\n\n\\subsection{SICA Pipeline}\n\\label{sec:pipeline}\n\nSymbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) extends SC by extracting formal constraints from traces and selecting the answer best supported by the satisfiable subset (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\n\n\\paragraph{Trace generation.} Given question $q$, an LLM generates $K$ traces $\\{t_1, \\ldots, t_K\\}$ at temperature $T = 0.7$. Each trace $t_i$ yields answer $a_i$.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint extraction.}\\label{sec:constraint-extraction} The same LLM extracts FOL constraints from each trace at $T_{\\text{ext}} = 0.3$. The extraction prompt requests JSON output with constraint type, expression, and Z3-compatible formula (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompts}). The \\emph{extraction rate} is the fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable constraint.\n\n\\paragraph{Deduplication and MAX-SAT.} Constraints are deduplicated by syntactic equivalence; each unique constraint $c_m$ receives weight $w_m$ equal to the number of source traces. The objective finds the largest-weight satisfiable subset:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:maxsat}\n\\mathcal{C}^* = \\arg\\max_{\\mathcal{S} \\subseteq \\mathcal{C}} \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{S}} w_c \\quad \\text{s.t.} \\quad \\mathcal{S} \\text{ is satisfiable.}\n\\end{equation}\nZ3 \\citep{demoura2008z3} solves this with a 10-second timeout. Answer assertion constraints ($\\texttt{answer\\_a} == \\texttt{True}$, weight 1) ensure every candidate receives a nonzero base score, making SICA a strict generalization of SC.\n\n\\paragraph{Scoring.} Let $\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus} = \\mathcal{C} \\setminus \\mathcal{C}^*$. Each candidate $a$ is scored by:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:score}\n\\text{score}(a) = \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^*} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a) - \\alpha \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\sigma(c, a) = 1$ if $c$ was extracted from any trace producing answer $a$, and $\\alpha = 0.5$. The selected answer is $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a} \\text{score}(a)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Degeneration condition.} If self-extracted constraints are coupled to their source answer, MAX-SAT retains nearly all ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| \\approx 0$), and scoring degenerates to:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:degenerate}\n\\text{score}(a) \\approx \\sum_{\\substack{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^* \\\\ \\text{source}(c) \\to a}} w_c,\n\\end{equation}\nequivalent to majority voting. We show in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias} that this degeneration occurs in practice.\n\n\\paragraph{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} The multi-candidate rate $C_0 = |\\{q : |\\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\\}| / |\\mathcal{Q}|$ is a zero-cost diagnostic computable before extraction. When $C_0 < 5\\%$, SICA produces the same output as SC.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n    node distance=0.4cm and 0.35cm,\n    box/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, minimum height=0.6cm,\n                text width=1.6cm, align=center, font=\\scriptsize},\n    arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick},\n    darr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick, densely dashed},\n    lbl/.style={font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!70!black},\n    ibottle/.style={draw=red!70!black, densely dashed, rounded corners=3pt,\n                    inner sep=3pt},\n]\n\\node[box] (q) {Question $q$};\n\\node[box, right=of q] (sample) {Sample $K$\\\\traces};\n\\node[box, right=of sample] (extract) {Extract FOL\\\\constraints};\n\\node[box, right=of extract] (dedup) {Dedup.\\ +\\\\weight};\n\\node[box, right=of dedup] (maxsat) {MAX-SAT\\\\(Z3)};\n\n\\node[box, below=0.7cm of maxsat, fill=gray!12] (ans) {Answer $a^*$};\n\\node[box, below=0.7cm of sample, fill=gray!12] (sc) {Majority\\\\vote};\n\n\\draw[arr] (q) -- (sample);\n\\draw[arr] (sample) -- (extract);\n\\draw[arr] (extract) -- (dedup);\n\\draw[arr] (dedup) -- (maxsat);\n\\draw[arr] (maxsat) -- (ans);\n\\draw[arr] (sample) -- node[lbl, right, yshift=0pt] {SC} (sc);\n\n\\node[lbl, above=0.01cm of maxsat] {SICA};\n\n\\node[ibottle, fit=(sample)(extract), label={[font=\\scriptsize\\itshape, text=red!70!black]above:Independence Bottleneck}] {};\n\n\\draw[darr, gray!60] ([xshift=-0.1cm]dedup.south) -- ++(0,-0.35) node[lbl, below, yshift=-0.05cm] {oracle: gold FOL};\n\\end{tikzpicture}\n\\caption{The SICA pipeline. SC selects the majority answer from $K$ traces. SICA extracts FOL constraints, deduplicates, and solves MAX-SAT via Z3. Red dashed box: the independence bottleneck. Dashed arrow: oracle variant with gold FOL.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{The Symbolic Mirage Hypothesis}\n\\label{sec:mirage}\n\nWe hypothesize that self-extracted symbolic structures are not independent verification signals but projections of the generating model's distributional biases. Formally, let $\\mathcal{E}_\\theta(t_i)$ denote constraints extracted by model $f_\\theta$ from trace $t_i$ with answer $a_i$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mirage}\nP\\bigl(\\mathcal{E}_\\theta(t_i) \\mid a^*, a_i\\bigr) \\neq P\\bigl(\\mathcal{E}_\\theta(t_i) \\mid a^*\\bigr),\n\\end{equation}\ni.e., extracted constraints depend on the model's answer $a_i$ given truth $a^*$. This \\textbf{Symbolic Mirage} is specific to self-extracted constraints; symbolic methods employing independently sourced constraints remain effective (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}). The mirage degrades aggregation through two channels: correlated constraints compress $K_{\\text{eff}} < 2$ (\\emph{direct}; Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}), and answer-coupled constraints collapse MAX-SAT to vote counting (\\emph{indirect}; Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). The bias ratio $\\text{BR} = P(\\text{support} \\mid \\text{wrong}) / P(\\text{support} \\mid \\text{correct})$ quantifies mirage intensity. Three testable predictions follow: (1)~BR $> 1.0$ across architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}); (2)~no same-model intervention reduces BR below 1.0 (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}); (3)~only external verification escapes the mirage (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n",

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Test-time computation scaling through multiple reasoning samples has emerged as a practical alternative to scaling model parameters \citep{snell2025scaling, guo2025deepseekr1}.
4	The dominant aggregation strategy, Self-Consistency (SC) \citep{wang2023sc}, samples $K$ chain-of-thought traces and selects the majority answer.
5	SC is simple, model-agnostic, and effective---but it is hitting a ceiling.
6	\citet{loo2025reevaluating} demonstrate that gains from increasing $K$ plateau and even decline for strong models.
7	The gap between SC's theoretical promise (independent correct voters converge to truth) and its practical behavior (saturating gains) points to a structural mismatch: same-model traces carry correlated errors, and majority voting has no mechanism to correct shared bias.
8	
9	Confidence-weighted \citep{taubenfeld2025cisc} and universal \citep{chen2024usc} extensions alleviate this, but the core limitation persists.
10	Recent neuro-symbolic methods \citep{pan2023logiclm, olausson2023linc, xu2026dylos, li2025aristotle, wang2025fover, luo2025logicagent} report gains on logical reasoning but introduce components beyond the reasoning model.
11	A simpler question precedes these: can the \emph{same} model extract useful formal constraints from its own traces---a zero-cost upgrade requiring no additional model or data?
12	No prior work has systematically evaluated this.
13	
14	We ground our analysis in the Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \citep{condorcet1785}: if $K$ independent voters each answer correctly with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth \citep{austensmith1996}.
15	When independence fails \citep{ladha1992correlated}, additional samples carry diminishing information.
16	Measuring inter-trace agreement with Fleiss' $\kappa$ \citep{fleiss1971kappa}, we find $\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ across instruction-tuned conditions, yielding Effective-$K = 1.20$--$3.62$ for nominal $K = 12$: traces beyond a dozen add negligible independent information (\S\ref{sec:effective-k}).
17	The bottleneck runs deeper than answer-level agreement suggests.
18	Chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level $\kappa$ ($0.41$), revealing that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on final answers (\S\ref{sec:process-kappa}).
19	When the same model extracts FOL constraints from these correlated traces, the constraints exhibit \emph{symbolic confirmation bias}: on problems where the model errs, 83.3\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\times$), and 94\% of errors overlap with SC (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).
20	Self-extraction cannot supply the independent signal that CJT requires.
21	
22	We study this through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA): extract FOL constraints from each of $K$ reasoning traces, deduplicate by syntactic equivalence, and select the answer best supported by a weighted partial MAX-SAT solution via Z3 \citep{demoura2008z3} (\S\ref{sec:pipeline}; Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}).
23	We evaluate SICA across six models (7B--32B), three reasoning domains (FOLIO \citep{han2022folio}, ProofWriter \citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}, LogiQA \citep{liu2020logiqa}), and over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\sim}$1,250 unique instances) with four-seed replication (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}).
24	Twenty-plus remediation strategies spanning fifteen families---from prompt engineering through method-level redesign---all produce neutral or negative results, confirming the bottleneck is persistent at the 7B--32B scale.
25	
26	Oracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound: same-model gold FOL yields $+29.90$\,pp (Mistral-7B) and $+14.22$\,pp (Qwen-14B) on FOLIO ($p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only ${\sim}$12\% of the same-model ceiling (\S\ref{sec:oracle}).
27	The central finding is a persistent independence-accuracy trade-off: Debate reduces $\kappa$ from 0.44 to 0.15 (tripling Eff-$K$) but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\,pp; SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG all cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$, forming a same-model ceiling that holds across three model families (\S\ref{sec:remediation}).
28	A cross-architecture proof-of-concept suggests this gap is recoverable: DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC yields $+3.50$\,pp on ProofWriter ($p = 0.013$, McNemar exact test) with consistent positive trends across datasets (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
29	
30	Our contributions are:
31	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
32	\item \textbf{Independence-Accuracy Trade-off.} Same-model $\kappa \geq 0.40$ across four methods and three model families; twenty-plus remediation strategies (fifteen families) fail. A cross-architecture NLI verifier ($+3.50$\,pp on ProofWriter, $p = 0.013$) suggests the ceiling is escapable (\S\ref{sec:remediation}, \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
33	
34	\item \textbf{Symbolic Confirmation Bias.} On incorrect problems, 83.3\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\times$), and 94\% of errors overlap with SC (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).
35	
36	\item \textbf{Process-Level Independence.} Across four model families, CoT cosine similarity ($0.69$--$0.79$) far exceeds answer-level $\kappa$ ($0.45$--$0.74$); the gap intensifies with model capability, showing reasoning homogeneity is architecture-independent (\S\ref{sec:process-kappa}).
37	\end{enumerate}
38	
```

models.\nThe gap between SC's theoretical promise (independent correct voters converge to truth) and its practical behavior (saturating gains) points to a structural mismatch: same-model traces carry
